**Verified live** on the two-spoke rig, exactly the post-cutover topology in miniature (spoke B as a hub for spoke A): a note written on A reached the continuous mirror in ~12s; a note written on A reached the **emergency** hub by push while A refused to read from it; a note written only on the emergency hub stayed invisible on A until approval, then arrived; the prompt fired at 24s against a 20s threshold carrying the failing hub's error; approving flipped the hub to pulling and the state back to synced; restoring the automatic hub auto-cleared the approval. The all-ask-first list and approving an already-automatic hub are both refused with reasons.

**Left open, recorded in ADR-210 rather than fixed:** foreign-origin ops are still not forwarded (a change that reached only one hub travels to the other via its own origin device, which holds in the star topology but not if that device is lost); a hub we push to but never pull from pins its own oplog (section C's problem, now reachable by configuration too); and the first pull after a long-unread archive is approved may hit ADR-208's 410 and want a re-fill. `cadence: "manual"` was not built — the two values cover the real topology.


### A2. ~~Performance and efficiency, done properly — local AND remote~~ — **DONE 2026-08-24 (ADR-215), one gate open: migration `0057` needs Tyler's ack (three additive `items` indexes; schema.md = core).**

The pass ran the way Brandon framed it: widen first, fix second, a number on everything. `scripts/perf-audit.mts` is the deliverable that outlives it — buffers + runtime per standard surface via EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS), read-only by construction, pointed at the live prod spoke, the cloud pooler, and the rig (`--url`, `--json`). **The widened measurement found two structural O(all-items) queries A2 didn't know about:** the related panel on EVERY item-page open (26,653 buffers — the OR-join gave the planner no index path, so it scanned and sorted all 23k items), and `mostLinked` all-types at 122,194 buffers. Both are rewritten (edges-first UNION ALL; a single grouped aggregate join): 26,653 → 3,241 and 122,194 → 3,448 buffers on the prod spoke, behavior suites passing byte-identical results. **The denormalized relation-count column is raised in ADR-215 and recommended AGAINST** — the rewrite makes the lens cost ~any other lens with no schema change, no trigger on every relation write, no counter that can drift.

The rest, all measured before/after: **nothing indexed `updated_at` at all**, so every list surface seq-scanned ~3,015 heap pages per render — migration `0057` adds two partial live-list indexes (`desc nulls last`, matching listOrderExpr byte-for-byte — a plain `desc` index does NOT satisfy it, measured) plus a trigram GIN for the per-keystroke picker ILIKE: recent lens 1,290 → 203 buffers, all-types → 11, picker 30ms → 0.5ms, badge counts → 29 as index-only scans. **Local Postgres is RAM-sized automatically** (`tunePostgres`, supervisor + restore cluster both): shared_buffers RAM/8 clamped 128MB–1GB so the whole 798MB database stays cached, rpc 1.1, work_mem 16MB; off-switch and manual overrides that always win. **Both restore paths end with VACUUM ANALYZE** (the never-analyzed planner believed 23,470 items were 7; stats alone re-planned the related panel 11× on the rig). **getSettings/listTypes wrapped in React cache()** — Nav + page each re-read both per navigation, two extra HTTP round trips per click on neon-http. Cloud measured too (read-only): same structural costs, so the rewrites and indexes pay there — the one cloud-distinctive number is the 4.5s first search after idle (Neon cold TOAST reads), accepted per runbook §5. Guards: `verify-supervisor.mts` +19, eleven reintroduced bugs each confirmed to fail. Runbook §5 got the durable rules.

**Rollout:** `0057` moves the journal tag, so ADR-206's version gate pauses sync between peers until each takes the update (same as ADR-213's rollout).

**Brandon's framing, and it is the point: be VERY THOROUGH.** Brainstorm many ways to improve speed and efficiency across both the local peers and the cloud deployment, get creative, and treat it as one deliberate pass rather than fixing whatever was last stumbled over. The measurements below are the starting evidence, not the scope.

**How this was found.** On a local spoke holding real production data, `/list/task` loaded fine on the default "recent" lens, but switching to "Most linked" took several seconds — while the same lens on Vercel/Neon was near-instant. The expectation was the opposite: local should be faster.

**What was measured (2026-08-23, on the prod spoke's local Postgres, 798MB database, 23,470 items / 18,780 relations). Do not re-derive:**

| Lens on `/list/task` | Buffers touched | Warm runtime |
| --- | --- | --- |
| recent (`updated_at`) | 3,082 | 24ms |
| title | 3,082 | 14ms |
| **Most linked** | **23,379** | 48ms |

- **`shared_buffers` is 128MB = 16,384 buffers.** One execution of Most linked touches **23,379** — more than the whole cache holds, so it evicts itself every run and can never stay warm. That is arithmetic, not a theory, and it is the primary cause.
- **Statistics had NEVER been gathered on the restored copy**: `last_analyze` / `last_autoanalyze` / `last_autovacuum` all NULL, so the planner believed `items` held **7 rows** (real: 23,470) and `relations` **0** (real: 18,780). A manual `ANALYZE` (2.8s) dropped the estimated cost 158k → 56k. It did not change the warm runtime — this query is I/O-bound, not plan-bound — but every other plan on that database was being chosen blind. **`local-restore.mjs` never analyzes**, which is the actual bug there.
- **`random_page_cost` is 4**, the spinning-disk default, so the planner systematically overprices the index scans these queries depend on. On SSD it should be ~1.1.
- `work_mem` 4MB; `items` is 454MB and `revisions` 170MB on this instance.
- **The lever is easy:** `embedded-postgres` accepts `postgresFlags: string[]`, so tuning is a `supervisor/config.json` block passed through `assembleAppEnv`'s sibling, not a code redesign.

**Candidate directions — raw material for the brainstorm, deliberately NOT a plan:**
- **Local Postgres tuning as a first-class part of the supervisor**: `shared_buffers` sized from the machine's RAM rather than a constant, `random_page_cost` for SSD, `work_mem`, `effective_cache_size`. Sized *automatically* is better than a number in a config file nobody revisits.
- **`ANALYZE` (and a first `VACUUM`) at the end of every fill path** — restore, `--from-url`, and after a large import. Cheap, and its absence is silent.
- **`mostLinked` is O(every matching row) on any backend** — a correlated `count(*)` per candidate. The cloud's larger cache hides it. A maintained relation count (denormalized column, or a trigger-updated counter) would make it O(limit). That is a schema change and wants its own ADR; it is also the only item here that helps the CLOUD as much as local.
- **The other lenses and views** deserve the same buffer-count treatment — this was found by accident on one lens, so measure them all rather than assuming the rest are fine.
- **The cloud side has its own axis**: the neon-http driver is hundreds of round trips per batch (the measured 8.46s for a 500-op sync batch is a *cloud* number). Where does that dominate outside sync?
- **A repeatable way to measure**, so this is not re-derived by hand next time: a script that reports buffers touched and runtime for the standard surfaces, local vs cloud, and can be re-run after a change.
- Revisit the runbook §5 performance rules against what is actually observed, rather than trusting they are still the right rules.

**Why it is queued rather than done:** found late on 2026-08-23 while Brandon was using the local spoke for real work. Nothing here is blocked by the Vercel build cap (all local), it is queued because it deserves one thorough pass instead of three reflexive patches.

### B. Install and onboarding a non-technical owner (Brandon, 2026-08-23)

The goal Brandon stated: **the install should not be technical, and where it unavoidably is, a locally-installed AI agent should be able to do it for the owner.** Three sub-items.

#### B1. ~~Tell the owner exactly what to type, and show them their own address~~ — **DONE 2026-08-23 (ADR-212).**

Both halves, and the second one is the part that actually removed the guesswork. **At the wizard's hub-URL prompt**, the help branches on what this machine can reach with, detected by shelling out to `tailscale status --json` rather than asking the owner to interpret anything: running → the tailnet form with this machine's own tailnet named; installed but not signed in → say `tailscale up` rather than offer an address that will not answer; absent → what to install and why, plus the alternatives *with their limits*. It prefers the MagicDNS hostname over the raw `100.x` (both work; the hostname is readable and survives a re-address). **On the hub**, Build → Network now shows "Other devices reach this instance at" — best first, Copy button on each, each labelled with what it does and does not reach — so adding a spoke is copy-from-one-screen, paste-into-another.

Two things worth keeping: a stopped or unsigned tailnet **publishes nothing** (a confidently-wrong address is worse than none), and Tailscale's own 100.64.0.0/10 range is filtered out of the LAN row, which otherwise duplicated the tailnet row under a label that said "only works on this network" — a lie. The Tailscale/Funnel split is stated in both surfaces rather than buried: a device that can join the tailnet needs no Funnel at all, and the only caller that really needs it is the claude.ai MCP connector, because that fetch comes from Anthropic's servers.

**A bug found on the way in:** `spawnSync("tailscale", …)` returns ENOENT on Windows even when the CLI *is* on PATH, because spawn does no PATHEXT resolution — so the first run reported "Tailscale is not installed" on a machine sitting on a tailnet. Both call sites ask for `tailscale.exe` on win32 now, guarded.

**Still open:** Funnel itself is deliberately not detected (its state is not cheaply readable, it is a separate step, and pretending to know would be worse than saying so), and the wizard does not yet print the address list at the end of a *hub* install — the Network page carries it, which covers the copy-paste case.


#### B2. ~~"Start when Windows starts"~~ — **DONE 2026-08-23 (ADR-211).**

The checkbox exists in both places Brandon asked for: the wizard asks (with `--startup=logon|always|none` for an unattended install), and the app carries the same toggle at **Build → Updates → Start with the computer**. **Logon vs always is now a real choice** rather than an accident — `--logon` maps to `/SC ONLOGON` and needs no elevation but waits for a sign-in; `--always` maps to `/SC ONSTART`, which is what a hub needs and generally wants an elevated prompt plus a stored credential. An absent or unrecognized scope resolves to `logon`, **never** to the one that demands elevation, which is guarded in both directions. `/RU` is passed only for the always-on scope.

The in-app toggle reuses the update path's signal file exactly as the section said to: the app writes `startup-requested`, the supervisor acts and records the outcome in `startup-state.json`. **A failure is a normal state here and is shown as one** — the reason plus the exact command to run in an Administrator prompt — because an owner who ticks a box and is not told it failed believes their hub survives a reboot.

The verbs exist: `npm run local:status` (supervisor alive? app actually answering? which build? registered at boot, read back from Windows rather than from our own record? — plus `--json` and an exit code an install agent can branch on), `npm run local:stop`, and `npm run local:startup` to read or change the registration later. They live in `supervisor/ledgr-ctl.mjs` because the supervisor itself boots Postgres and the app on run and so cannot answer a question without becoming the thing being asked about.

**The rig found two more bugs here.** First, `stop` reported success but left a stale lock, because a termination signal sent to another process on Windows is a hard kill Node cannot catch — the shutdown handler never ran, so Postgres was killed rather than shut down and the lock survived looking like a live owner. `stop` now asks through a `stop-requested` file that reaches the same handler a Ctrl-C reaches. Second, even *with* the handler running, every restart replayed WAL: **embedded-postgres stops the cluster with `taskkill /f /t`**, which is a kill, not a shutdown. `shutdown()` now asks `pg_ctl stop -m fast` first and keeps the forced path as a bounded fallback; verified by a stop logging `clean: true` and the next start showing zero recovery lines.

**Still open, deliberately:** writing and enabling the **launchd plist / systemd user unit** the same way win32 does its `schtasks` (both are still printed for hand-installation, and the tooling says so rather than pretending), `install.sh` for the non-Windows bootstrap, and the supervisor still not restarting *itself* when an update changes `supervisor/*.mjs` (listed in E; the new verbs are what make that fix testable). Note for whoever tests the registration on a fresh machine: **this machine's account cannot create scheduled tasks unelevated at all** — both scopes return "Access is denied" — so the honest-failure path is what got exercised end to end here, not a successful registration.


#### B3. A locally-installed AI agent can stand up a hub or a spoke

The target: the owner runs Claude Code (or similar) on the new machine and it completes the install, stopping only where a human genuinely must act. What blocks that today, all of it already queued below in E but re-framed here as one goal:

- **`/api/machine/bootstrap`** — until it exists, filling a new peer needs a production connection string or a dump file, neither of which an agent should be handling. With it, a device token is the only input. CORE, needs an ADR and Tyler.
- **MCP tools for sync devices** (`list_sync_devices` / `add_sync_device` / `revoke_sync_device`) — minting a token needs a browser Clerk session today, which is the one step an agent cannot do. Additive, solo-shippable. Keep the deliberate limit: the MCP tool mints **pull-only** devices only.
- **`--sync-mode` flag on the wizard** so an unattended install can choose the safe posture.
- **A `status` verb** (B2) so the agent can verify rather than assume.

**Name the irreducibly-human steps explicitly, so the agent knows to stop and ask rather than improvise:** (1) the Tailscale sign-in, and enabling the Funnel node attribute in the Tailscale admin console; (2) the first Clerk browser sign-in on a hub that serves a public surface; (3) the Windows elevation prompt and stored credential for run-when-logged-out; (4) pasting a device token, until B3's bootstrap + MCP work removes it. A written list of four is the acceptance test for "the install is not technical" — anything else the agent hits is a gap worth fixing.

### C. The sleeping-spoke remainder — paused devices and the return path

The **staleness refusal is built and merged** (ADR-208, PR #302), so a peer that has been away too long is now refused with a 410 and told a re-fill is required, rather than being handed a partial database and reporting itself synced. Verified live against the dev hub. Two thirds of the original problem remain:

- [x] ~~**A registered sleeping peer pins the hub's oplog indefinitely**, and **revoking to park a device is what destroys its ability to resume.**~~ **BOTH FIXED 2026-08-23 (ADR-213).** `hold_mode` per device (`auto` | `warm` | `cold`) governs **retention only**, leaving access to `revoked` and `pull_only` — separating those axes was the fix, since conflating them is what made "park it" and "let it come back" mutually exclusive. `auto` is the default and bounds the oplog at one retention window (14 days, per-device) with nobody having to remember anything; `warm` is a deliberate unbounded hold for a machine you know is coming back; `cold` releases now. **Going cold is announced rather than discovered:** at 70% of the window (a fraction, so it scales with a longer one) Synced devices names the device, says what happens if it does not reconnect, and offers both answers inline. The same bug in different clothes also closed: a token minted and never used sat at cursor 0 and pinned the *entire* oplog, so the clock is `last_seen_at` falling back to `created_at`.

  **Measured while designing it, worth keeping:** on a copy of production (10,372 items) the full row an op carries averages **8.3 KB** (p90 14 KB, max 1.9 MB), so a 14-day window is ~12 MB and an unbounded hold grows ~**25 MB/month**. That is what made Brandon's "let the owner keep something warm" affordable rather than reckless — and it says the real risk is a body-heavy spike (one 931 KB item writes a ~931 KB op *per save*), not normal typing.

  **The parked prod spoke** (data at `C:\ledgr-data-parked`, device deliberately not revoked) stops pinning production's oplog roughly two weeks after it was parked, and the Devices list will warn before it does. If it is meant to come back, set it to **keep its history** first.
- **The middle sync mode (reconcile-what-differs) is designed but not built** — full design in ADR-208, with both traps recorded: (a) "differs" must **not** become "the hub wins" (a differing row still resolves by the same per-field LWW as ordinary sync, so the reconcile payload has to carry per-field stamps or the rows have to merge through `mergeOps` exactly as ops do); (b) any row digest must **exclude generated and derived columns** (`items.search` is generated, `passage_refs` is derived), which is why the v1 digest is `(tbl, id, updated_at)`. A full re-fill stays the fallback and is fast (~4 minutes for 576k rows, measured), so the system's job is to *know* a re-fill is needed and offer it as a button, not to avoid it.

### D. Phase 5 hub duties, then phase 6 cutover

The remaining ADR-206 phases. **Local crons landed 2026-08-23 (ADR-214), so what is left here genuinely needs Brandon's machine and accounts rather than code:**

- **Expose the PC** (B1's Funnel work) and confirm a real HTTPS URL.
- **Point the accounts at it:** a claude.ai MCP connector and a Clerk phone sign-in against that URL. This was the old "probe 3" and it cannot be automated.
- ~~**Local crons.**~~ **DONE 2026-08-23 (ADR-214).** The seam already existed at the HTTP layer — Vercel cron and GitHub Actions both just `GET /api/machine/<job>` with a cron-scoped token — so what was missing was a **trigger**, not an interface. The supervisor is it: one 60s timer beside its existing signal polls, calling the same endpoints over loopback, authenticated by a cron token it **mints for itself per process** (in memory, only the sha256 appended to the app child's `LEDGR_API_TOKENS`, never on disk), so there is nothing to configure and no second auth path. Which jobs run is a written table with a double-run verdict per job, because a local peer runs alongside a cloud deployment still running all of them: **`purge` and `relatedness` default on** (the first because `pruneSyncOps` only ever prunes the oplog of the instance it runs on — the gap this item existed for; the second because `item_relatedness` is a per-instance cache, so Discover and Loose Ends stay empty until a peer fills its own), and **`export`, `calendar-sync`, `email-import`, `todoist-sync`, `transcription-poll`, `health-check` are off until asked for**, each writing somewhere shared. Schedules are `at: "HH:MM"` local or `everyMinutes` — no cron parser, since the workflows' weekday shaping exists only to save Neon compute. A failure is recorded in `cron-state.json`, retried in 10 min clamped to the next slot, and **POSTed to `/api/machine/report-error` exactly as the GitHub workflows do**, so it lands in `error_log` and counts on `/health`. Surfaced three ways: Build → Updates → "Scheduled jobs on this machine", `npm run local:status` (`--json` included), and the state file itself, written at startup so "no jobs here" never looks like "no supervisor". A job overdue by more than its period runs 3 min after boot, so a laptop asleep at 03:10 every night still purges. **Verified live on the dev rig** (spoke B; the prod spoke untouched): purge running through the minted token, export recording a real 503 with its detail and the `report-error` row in `error_log`, the retry clamped to the interval, and both rendering on the page and in `local:status`.
- **Ordered-hub failover** — which is section A.
- **Cutover (phase 6):** flip the MCP connector URL, flip the phone PWA URL, reorder the hub list so the cloud becomes the archive peer, **hand the exclusive jobs over** (turn `export` / `calendar-sync` / `email-import` on in the hub's `crons`, and stop the cloud running them — ADR-214 keeps them off by default precisely so this is a deliberate step rather than a collision), write the runbook entry. Reversible by reversing the same flips; the cloud keeps its copy either way (ADR-206 decision 4). Blobs stay on R2 through cutover (decision 8), so there is no blob work here.

### E. Smaller open items

- [ ] **What to test about the CLOUD hub as a backup, specifically (Brandon, 2026-08-23 — raised, deferred).** ADR-210's fallback path was proven with one local spoke acting as another's hub, which is the post-cutover topology in miniature and exercised every code path. What it did *not* exercise is the cloud peer in the emergency-hub seat, where the differences are environmental rather than logical: the 60s function ceiling on a drained catch-up after a long push-only stretch, whether a daily-cadence archive reliably trips ADR-208's 410 when the owner finally approves pulling from it (it should, and the prompt already shows how far behind it is), and Neon cold-start latency inflating a first exchange. None of these change the design; they are worth one deliberate rehearsal before relying on the cloud as the emergency hub after cutover.
- [ ] **Oplog size is the one large-import obstacle still unfixed.** ADR-206's wire format puts the whole changed row in `changed`, bodies included, so importing 500MB of bodies writes ~500MB of oplog on the spoke, pushes it, and grows the hub's oplog by the echo too, all held for the 14-day retention. On Neon's free tier that is worth knowing before, not after. The other two obstacles are done: the first-push hold now has a one-click GUI release (PR #305) and throughput was measured at 8.46s for a full 500-op batch (14% of the 60s ceiling), so **importing on a spoke is now a fine default** — the old "import on the hub instead" advice was conditional on those two and no longer applies, except for a genuinely huge body-heavy import where the oplog cost argues for the hub.
- [ ] **The update signal's target sha is decorative.** `/api/updates` writes the target commit into `update-requested`, and `applyUpdate` reads it only for the log message: it then does `git pull --ff-only origin <branch>` and builds whatever HEAD becomes. Benign when HEAD is that commit, wrong when origin has moved on — the owner consents to the commits the card showed and can get a newer set. Either honor it (build the target when it is an ancestor of the pulled HEAD, else refuse) or stop writing it.
- [ ] **The supervisor reads `branch` once, at start, so a peer pointed at a merged-and-deleted branch refuses every update until someone restarts it.** Hit twice in the 2026-08-23 evening session, from opposite ends: a rig spoke was moved onto a feature branch to test it, the branch was merged (and deleted on the remote), and every subsequent update signal failed with `fatal: couldn't find remote ref <branch>` — editing `branch` back to `main` in `supervisor/config.json` did nothing until the process was restarted. Keep-last-good handled it correctly (the peer kept serving its previous build and said why in the log), so this is friction rather than danger, but it is exactly the friction the mode toggle and the GUI hub list removed elsewhere: **config the app can change should not need a restart.** Two candidate fixes, either cheap: re-read the config's `branch` per update attempt (it is already re-read from disk nowhere, but nothing depends on it being stable), or fall back to `origin/HEAD` with a warning when the configured ref is gone. Pairs with the item below, since both are "the supervisor is holding stale state about itself."
- [ ] **The supervisor never restarts itself.** An update that changes `supervisor/*.mjs` flips the app but leaves the old supervisor running its old code until a manual restart, so supervisor fixes land a restart later than they look like they do. Documented in the README for now; pairs naturally with B2's `stop`/`status` verbs.
- [ ] **Resume the alongside soak.** The prod spoke is parked so its machine could host the dev rig (see C for the retention consequence). Unpark it when the rig is no longer needed: rename `C:\ledgr-data-parked` back and start the supervisor — its device was never revoked, so it can still resume, though it may now be far enough behind to hit the new 410 and need a re-fill.
- [x] ~~**The weekly backup was failing since 2026-08-14.**~~ **FIXED** — the chunked-fragment upload landed (`fix(backup): upload the weekly dump in fragments, not one PUT`) and two runs went green on 2026-08-23. The next *scheduled* run is the real proof, since both green runs were manual. The silence that hid it is a separate item: `LEDGR_ERROR_TOKEN` is still unset in Actions, so the report-error step exits 0 and `/health` showed `errors.last24h: 0` straight through two failures. **Set that secret** or the next silent failure is equally invisible.
- [x] ~~**`.env.local` can silently be production.**~~ **FIXED 2026-08-23 on this machine:** `.env.local` now points at the dev Neon branch (`ep-cold-smoke-ai4f98m1`, what devledgr serves) and `.env.production.local` holds the prod string, both verified by comparing `job_state` against each deployment's `/health`, and both pushed to the secrets store so every machine can pull them (the prod file had never been pushed before). `release:prod` can now complete from here. **The gotcha stands and is worth a guard:** `vercel env pull` overwrites `.env.local` with production values, so anyone running it re-breaks this silently. A guard in the `db:*` scripts that is honest about not being able to tell dev from prod by connection string alone is still unbuilt.

### Done in the 2026-08-23 sessions (for the record)

Bidirectional sync proven against production (LWW both directions, body conflicts to `revisions` with the flag, soft and hard delete propagating, echo terminating in one round on neon-http). Then, on a two-spoke dev rig against `devledgr` (spokes at `C:\ledgr-a` / `C:\ledgr-b`, ports 3000/5433 and 3002/5434, filled from the dev Neon branch): **ADR-208** the staleness refusal + push dedupe (PR #302, Tyler acked); **ADR-206 addendum 7** the FK family-delete wedge, which the rig caught within minutes of arming and which prod's trash purge would have hit on the first parent-with-children purge (PR #303); **ADR-209** Build → Network with a GUI hub list, a token per hub, and per-hub status (PR #304); the **"Send anyway"** one-click release for a held first push (PR #305). Guards proven live rather than only pure-tested: first-push hold (800 ops held, pull still flowing, released, drained), clock-skew hold (push held, pull flowing, drained on restore), three-node propagation with no echo growth, and a hub-cron write colliding with a spoke edit resolving per field. Throughput measured: **8.46s for a full 500-op batch** against Neon HTTP, 14% of the 60s ceiling.


## Done — Local hub/spoke LH3, the physical half: PC bring-up (Brandon, ADR-206)

**Complete.** The bring-up ran on two Windows machines (2026-08-22/23): git + Node, clone, `npm ci`, supervisor config, `local:restore`, Task Scheduler registration, reboot proof, then sync armed against the cloud hub. All three README watch-items came up clean in practice: no file-lock failure at a build swap across four keep-last-good flips, the embedded-postgres win32 binaries ran without antivirus intervention, and the firewall prompt appeared and was allowed as documented. The soak is the only part still owed, and it is tracked in the road-to-cutover block above (section E, "Resume the alongside soak") because the prod spoke is currently parked to free its machine for the dev rig. LH4 (the installer wizard) shipped; LH5 hub duties are section D above.

## Queued — Gantt Chart tool for projects (Tyler, 2026-08-18)

A "Gantt Chart" **tool** for project records (the ADR-199/200 tool model: a widget on the widget homepage + likely a full drill-in page, same shape as the review timeline). Bars from the dates the items already carry — tasks (scheduled → due), milestones (target dates) — so it's a pure read over existing data, no new columns. Open questions when picked up: bar for a task with only one date (point vs. bar), whether dragging a bar edits the date (pairs with `explorations/calendar-time-blocking.md`), and dependencies (almost certainly out of scope for v1 of the tool; Ledgr has no dependency edge today). Not core (module/tool internals) unless a dependency edge gets proposed — that would be a relations-contract conversation.

## Queued — Project-like types as tools on projects: the open discussion (Tyler, 2026-08-18; parts 1–2 built 2026-08-19, ADR-204)

From Tyler's "Project Purchases" live-use session. **Parts 1 and 2 are DONE on branch `feat/widget-home-opt-in` (ADR-204):** the widget homepage is now an explicit **Project-style page** checkbox on the type editor (off by default for new custom types; types created under the old default are grandfathered on, unchecking is the one-click off), and the custom-tool card's "+ Add" works — `/api/records/[id]/contain` accepts any live type instead of the built-in allowlist, and the button toasts instead of failing silently. Deliberately NOT built from part 1: a pick-your-starting-tools step when the checkbox flips on — the page starts from the standard composition and Add a Tool shapes it; add the picker later if the default composition annoys in practice. **Also on the branch (ADR-204 addendum):** every tool card's hover gear has **Rename** — a per-record card title stored as `options.title` on the widget instance, honored by the card header and its drill-down page; empty restores the default.

What remains is part 3:

**Project-like types as tools on projects — OPEN QUESTION, worth a real discussion (Brandon included) before building.** Tyler's lean (2026-08-18): allow it — "it feels in the spirit of Ledgr" that a type being hub-shaped shouldn't disqualify it from ALSO being a tool on another record ("I don't see why a project can't be related to another project type and have it as a tool anyway"). Hub-ness and tool-ness look orthogonal; the current guard refuses built-in tool types only where the card would double. Things the discussion should settle: recursion/containment semantics (a hub inside a hub — what does its card preview show, and does progress roll up?), whether the home edge or a plain relation carries it, and how it meets `explorations/project-items.md` / the relatedRecords box (a Pursuit's projects), which already half-supports nesting project-shaped things.

## Queued — Custom layouts vs. bespoke default canvases: reconcile (Tyler, 2026-08-18)

Saving a custom layout for a type with a bespoke canvas silently swaps the whole experience: `TaskCanvas` (and the project widget home) defer to the generic `MarkdownCanvas` grid whenever `typeDef.canvasLayout != null`, so one visit to "Customize layout" on task throws away the new rail-panel canvas (ADR-202) — working as designed (ADR-069 regression fix), but a rough trade the user never saw coming. Needs design thought, not just code. Options to weigh when picked up: (a) warn/preview at the moment of customizing a bespoke-canvas type ("this replaces the built-in task page") + a one-click "back to the built-in layout" affordance (partially exists — clearing the layout restores it, but nothing says so); (b) make bespoke canvases themselves arrangeable (big); (c) scope customize-layout away from bespoke types entirely (defer-by-hiding). Also check what the guide says about Customize layout. Related: the "Select" toggle discussion below in COLLAB — both are "the control works but doesn't explain itself" cases.

## Queued — Drag-to-reposition cropper for the person image (Tyler, 2026-08-18)

The person picture box (ADR-202 addendum 4, `src/components/people/PersonImageBox.tsx`) currently center-crops uploads to a square automatically. The follow-up: a small crop step between choosing the file and uploading — drag to reposition (and pinch/wheel to zoom) the square window over the image, then commit. No dependency (Principle 5): render the image in a fixed square viewport, track pointer drags as an offset, export through the same canvas `drawImage` the auto-crop already uses (only the source rect changes). Pasted URLs skip the cropper (we don't proxy remote bytes); it's upload-only. Worth also applying to any future image-kind property if one ever exists.

## Potential launch (Ledgr as a product/company)

**Not the build queue.** Long-horizon track from the 2026-08-14/15 Tyler+Claude sessions; the full reasoning, options, and phase triggers live in `explorations/onboarding-and-multi-tenancy.md` (§9–§12). Two markets identified: the fused notes-graph + full-task-manager combination nobody ships (§10a), and the ministry vertical nobody serves (§10b). Model: free self-host tier + paid hosted tier (the Ghost/Cal.com shape). **Everything here is gated on the Brandon conversation** — his involvement is a spectrum (partner → advisor → key user), all fine, but Phase 1 memorializes whichever he picks.

- [ ] **§6 setup quick wins** (first sign-in claims the instance, deploy button + Neon marketplace, `/setup` page, solo-auth adapter). Not core, helps every path, start whenever. Item 1 is the best value-to-effort.
- [ ] **Friends/family beta box** — N single-tenant instances on the home server (per-person database + app container, reverse proxy). Keeps Principle 7 intact. Free; its job is measuring support burden per non-technical user.
- [ ] **The Brandon conversation** (trigger: before any charging) — operating agreement or IP-assignment/advisor agreement depending where on the spectrum he lands; written blessing for using his name/story in marketing either way.
- [ ] **Name decision — stay Ledgr (the sixth Ledger) or rename: OPEN discussion, both-agree** — the collision picture is confirmed, and worse than the Ledger worry (Tyler + Claude web pass, 2026-08-24): the exact spelling "Ledgr" is already taken several times over in software — LEDGR Money holds the name on the iOS App Store (plus Android and useledgr.com), a Ledgr sits on the Microsoft Store, ledgr.health does medical credentialing, and a Ledgr bookkeeping service exists — all on top of Ledger SAS (hardware wallets, broad software marks, phonetically identical). The App Store name alone is a hard block on the bare name. Fine as the private name indefinitely; decide the public one before printing it anywhere. First-pass candidates from the same session (quick web checks only, NOT a USPTO/domain clearance): **Bindery** (a bindery is where loose pages become one bound book — "everything is an item" in one system; the most open name checked, nearest neighbors are small "Binder" note apps with no dominant in-category mark), **Carillon** (church bells calling people together; collides with Carillon ERP, accounting software since 1994, plus literal bell-system software — risky), **Cairn** (trail marker; crowded namespace, nothing dominant in-category). Checked and DEAD, don't re-propose: Commonplace (two active notes products, one on the App Store), Tessera (Tessera Layers is a nested notes/journal app, same category), Vestry (vestry.life is church management software — the collision is in the target vertical), Steward (a Mac personal-database app plus Good Steward finance). The safest lane long-term is a coined name; whatever the shortlist becomes, clear it against USPTO TESS + the .com + both app stores before committing. Both-agree — the public name is Brandon's call as much as Tyler's. **Tyler's current frontrunner (2026-08-24): Almanack** — the archaic-k spelling is the ownable part; the neighbor to clear is Almanac (no k), the former docs startup at almanac.io. Unvetted bench in the same old-book vein, for the eventual deep dive: Kalendar (church kalendar = liturgical calendar, same archaic-spelling trick), Daybook (the merchant's capture-everything-then-sort record — Ledgr's own flow, keeps the ledger heritage without the mark), Compendium, Omnibus, Colophon, Octavo, Marginalia, Concordance (the Bible's cross-reference index = the relations graph in one word), Breviary, Vade Mecum. **Where the discussion stands (Tyler, 2026-08-24): staying Ledgr is a live option, not a rejected one — decide with Brandon whether to be the sixth Ledger.** Tyler's read: personal-finance apps and life-management/productivity are different enough lanes that Ledgr fits the coexistence pattern the other Ledg(e)rs already prove out (trademark rights are per-lane, and "ledger" is a weak, crowded mark, so everyone's protection is thin); the *real* constraints are namespace, not law — the bare name is unavailable on the iOS App Store and the good domains are gone — and a second word ("Ledgr Notes"-style) plausibly solves exactly those two. Counterpoints on record so the decision weighs them instead of re-deriving them: (1) a generic second word carries almost no weight in a confusion analysis — the dominant element is still LEDGR, phonetically LEDGER; (2) shared distinctive word + different generic suffixes is what a product *family* looks like, so "Ledgr Notes" can read as LEDGR Money's sibling — and LEDGR Money is the one holder on the same consumer app-store shelf; (3) joining a crowded weak-mark field forfeits the moat both ways: the sixth Ledger can't stop the seventh, and every marketing dollar partly promotes the other five. Middle path also on record: a distinctive brand with "ledger" demoted to the tagline ("a ledger for your whole life"), keeping the concept while vacating the contested slot. **The lens for judging collisions (Tyler, 2026-08-24): relatedness = same product lane (personal life-management/productivity software), NOT "it's also software."** A font editor, an EPUB tool, or a crypto product is another industry; flags from other industries are identity/SEO considerations, not legal walls. The two carve-outs to still check at the deep dive: truly famous marks can reach across industries (dilution — Tether is the only candidate where that's conceivable), and consumer apps on the same app-store shelf get judged somewhat more closely. Under this lens the only near-lane flags on the list are almanac.io (docs tool, defunct) and Tome.app (presentations, adjacent). Tyler's candidate list if renaming (2026-08-24) — his picks, none eliminated; the parentheticals are Claude's collision-research notes to weigh at the deep dive, not verdicts: **Almanack** (frontrunner; neighbor to clear is almanac.io, the former docs startup), **Kept**, **Tether** (Tyler likes it — short, modern, feels similar to Ledgr, and the meaning fits the home-base-with-offshoots framing; flag: Tether is the USDT stablecoin company, the same crypto lane that makes Ledger SAS a concern), **Node / Nodal** (both feel right to Tyler — items-as-nodes matches the relations graph; flags: Node.js dominates the bare word in software, Nodal Exchange is a far-lane neighbor), **Slate** (double meaning: the slate you write on + "slated" = scheduled; flags: Slate magazine, Slate Auto, Slate Digital), **Sigil** (flags: Sigil the EPUB editor; current usage of the word leans occult/chaos-magic outside the GoT house-emblem sense — weigh against the ministry vertical), **Glyph** (confirmed liked; flags: Glyphs the Mac font editor, Glyph pro drives), **Tome** (flag: Tome.app, the AI presentation startup), **Nexa** (flags: a Nexa cryptocurrency, Nexa3D). If the ledger-lane question stays contested, a trademark attorney's clearance opinion (~$500–1,500) is the tiebreaker — worth buying only when launch gets real.
- [ ] **Pick a license** — the public repo has **no LICENSE file** (= all-rights-reserved; source-visible but nobody may legally run it). The free self-host tier requires a real one (AGPL à la Cal.com, or fair-source). Both-agree.
- [ ] **LLC + ToS + privacy policy + Stripe** (trigger: first dollar). Pastoral care notes make the privacy policy non-optional.
- [ ] **Control plane** (trigger: first stranger) — signup → provision DB → migrate → seed owner → subdomain, unattended. The self-update work (3fbe738) is groundwork. Tenancy call: managed single-tenant (DB per customer) vs true multi-tenant (`types` owner-scoping + ADR-075 re-argument).
- [ ] **ADR-075 revisit for hosted strangers** — care notes are the sensitive core of the pastor use case; possibly encryption-at-rest, written up as a public trust page (privacy is marketing in this vertical).
- [ ] **Church-market seeding** — 10–20 known pastors on free accounts (testimony = launch asset); content from Brandon's real workflows; free for planters/missionaries. Sell the week ("Sunday-proof, whole ministry in one place"), never the database.

> **✅ THE CHECK ITSELF IS NOW FIXED (2026-08-12, ADR-188).** Both "worth fixing the check itself" notes below are answered: **there was no CI workflow at all** — all ten workflows are operational crons — which is why two type errors merged green and broke every production deploy from `main`. `.github/workflows/ci.yml` now runs **typecheck → lint → build → the 51 pure verify suites** on PRs *and* on pushes to `main` (the second breakage came by direct push). **No secrets**, verified not assumed: the build passes without `DATABASE_URL` or a Clerk key, because the db client is lazy and ADR-184's keyless fallback keys on `VERCEL_ENV`, which Actions doesn't set. `npm run ci` runs the same thing locally. `scripts/verify-ci.mjs` **discovers** the pure suites rather than reading an allowlist, so a new one is covered the moment it lands.
>
> **And it immediately paid for itself: six verify scripts were already failing and nobody knew, because nothing ran them.** All six were **stale tests, not code bugs** — note that, because the instinct on a red test is to change the code. `verify-songs`/`verify-papers` expected note's canvas to be `markdown` (it's `longform`, ADR-157); `verify-build-mode` expected `/dashboards/<id>` to be a Build path (it deliberately isn't — claiming it once made Build mode the only way to view an unassigned dashboard, and the test now says so); `verify-provider-seams` called four `/api/machine` routes unauthenticated (they gate on the ADR-117 `verifyApiToken` shim — I read all four to confirm they 401 before loosening the guard, then made it **per handler**, stricter than before); `verify-graph-auth` could only pass on a machine holding real `GRAPH_*` secrets; `verify-markdown-escape` used the pre-palette Notion red `#e03e3e`, which `textColorName` rejects, so the span parsed to an **empty doc**.
>
> **⚠️ TWO FOLLOW-UPS THIS DOESN'T COVER.** (1) **`CI / check` is not yet a required status check** on `main` — that's a GitHub branch-protection setting, not a file, so it can't ride in a commit. Until someone sets it, **the gate reports but does not block a merge.** (2) The **79 DB/server-backed suites still have no runner** and may hold more stale expectations of exactly the kind found above — a local `verify:all` against a scratch database is the obvious next step, and `npm run verify:list` shows the split.
>
> **📍 SETTLED, not a problem — where the repo lives and who can see it (Tyler, 2026-08-12).** Ledgr is at **`strategicli/ledgr`**, owned by a GitHub **organization**: Brandon moved it off his personal account so he and Tyler can both push and pull. It is **public on purpose** — a private org repo costs money and they chose not to pay. No secrets are in the repo (they live in environment settings), so world-readable code is the standing assumption, not an oversight. GitHub still forwards the old `brandonscollins/ledgr` address, which is where the "This repository moved" line on every push came from; the local remote now points at the real one. **Don't raise any of this as a finding again.**

> **🔴 FIXED IN PASSING AGAIN — `main` did not build, same failure mode as PR #214 (2026-08-12).** `src/app/page.tsx` carried a second named export, `export async function TodayHome()`, which `/today` imported (`import { TodayHome } from "@/app/page"`). A Next.js **page file may only export from a fixed allowlist** (`default`, `dynamic`, `metadata`, `generateMetadata`, …), so the extra export failed the generated route typecheck — and `next build` runs the typecheck, so **every Vercel deploy from `main` was failing**. Extracted to `src/app/_today-home.tsx` (the `_` prefix keeps it out of the router) as the default export; `/` and `/today` both import it from there. `tsc` 0 errors, `next build` ✓, both routes 200. **This is the second time an extra-export/type error has merged green — the pre-merge check still evidently does not run `tsc`/`next build`.** Worth fixing the check itself, not just the third occurrence.

> **▶️ START HERE (2026-08-13) — everything below through ADR-190 is MERGED AND DEPLOYED.** `fix/punch-list-2026-08-12` landed on `main` in `ce2218c` on 2026-08-12, and `main` has since moved to `4446ac9` with Brandon's three PRs of 2026-08-13 (#251 editor toolbar tooltips name their shortcuts, #252 the PWA share-target cold-share fix / ADR-191, #253 inline HTML spans surviving an ordered-list round-trip). **Both instances build Production straight from `main`**, so all of it is live on Brandon's deploy and Tyler's; `origin/prod-brandon` and `origin/main` are the same commit, and `origin/prod-tyler` has been deleted. CI on `main` is green.
>
> **⏭️ THE OPEN WORK IS FOUR DECISIONS AND ONE UNVERIFIED-ON-HARDWARE ITEM, not a build queue:**
> 1. **Make `CI / check` a required status check** on `main` (GitHub branch protection — a repo setting, not a file, so it can't ride in a commit). Until someone sets it the gate reports but does not block, and two type errors have already merged green and broken every production deploy.
> 2. **PR #140, the deploy-workflow proposal** (`main` = integration + per-instance prod branches), open since 2026-07-01 and sharper now that both instances deploy Production from `main`: any merge reaches Brandon's live data within a minute.
> 3. **`tyler/user-defined-api-keys`** — 2 commits from 2026-06-15 (ADR-067 per-type-scoped API keys, Build → API page, +2,552 lines) carrying migration `0018` that never landed anywhere. Revive or delete the branch.
> 4. **`purge-throwaway-owners.mjs --apply`** — see the 🧹 item below; still not run, still your call because it hard-deletes.
>
> **The three punch-list items still not clicked through on real hardware** (no browser automation in those sessions; they're covered by pure/structural guards, the served CSS, and live-DB calls, which is not the same as eyes on it). Now that they're deployed, each is worth 60 seconds on the live app: type `@` + a new name in a body and confirm the three create rows; open the `@` picker with the ⋯ kebab already open and confirm the kebab closes; add a task in a project's Tasks card and confirm the row stops saying "Adding…" almost immediately.

> **✅ DONE (2026-08-13, ADR-192, CORE additive) — `phone` and `email` property kinds.** The backlog item below is built, and **its premise was half wrong in a way worth keeping**: `CustomProperties.tsx` already rendered `tel:`/`mailto:` links and the right keyboard, but it guessed the intent from the field's **key** (`/phone|mobile|\btel\b|cell/`), so `mobile_2` got a dialer and `contact_line` did not, for no reason the owner could see — and only on the record canvas, never in a list. So this replaced a guess with a declaration rather than adding a missing feature.
>
> - **Storage is untouched:** both kinds are `text`, the value is stored **exactly as typed**, and nothing validates or reformats it. No migration, no backfill. An existing `text` field keeps behaving as it did until someone changes its kind.
> - **The non-obvious bug, found by writing the doc comment before the code:** stripping non-digits turns `(309) 555-0142 x12` into `tel:309555014212`, and the extension digits read as more of the number, so it **misdials**. `telHref` now cuts anything past a `,`/`;` and a trailing `x|ext|extension` first. The extension stays on screen to be entered after the call connects. DTMF `,,12` was rejected (pause support varies by dialer; a wrong pause misdials silently too).
> - **The legacy key heuristic stays, scoped to `text`,** because production data — the Notion import, Tyler's people — is full of contact fields typed `text`, and retyping them all is not a cost this change gets to impose. A declared kind always wins.
> - **List linking is the table layout only:** the compact row wraps the whole row in a `Link` to the item, so an anchor inside it is invalid HTML *and* would steal the row tap. `ViewRenderer` took an optional `propertyKinds` map (same shape and resolution sites as `propertyLabels`, defaulted `{}` so unadopted callers render identically).
> - **Filtering is text filtering** over the raw value, so a stored `(309) 555-0142` does not match a `3095550142` search. Making it match needs a normalized shadow value nothing writes — noted, not built.
> - `tsc` + `eslint` + `next build` clean. New `scripts/verify-contact-links.mts` (**66 checks, ALL PASS**, pure, so `verify-ci.mjs` discovered it with no allowlist edit — 55 pure suites now). `verify-types.mts` updated for the count and green against the dev DB.
> - **✅ CONFIRMED WORKING (Tyler, 2026-08-13, on the deployed instance).** The `person` type carries `phone`/`email` kinds in production (read back over MCP, so the type editor → Postgres → read path is proven end to end), and **both links were clicked on desktop and worked**. Also verified against the real stored values through the shipped helper: `tel:6073495980`, `mailto:tyler@bethanycentral.org`, with the `tel`/`email` keyboards. **Two things that value can't exercise, noted rather than claimed:** it's stored as bare digits, so neither the punctuation-stripping nor the extension cut was actually hit — set a field to `(607) 349-5980 x12` if you ever want to see the misdial guard work — and a desktop click is not a phone dialer.
> - **Found while verifying: the `organization` type's `phone`/`email` are still kind `text`.** They work anyway, via the legacy key heuristic (the back-compat path, confirmed working), but only because those keys happen to be named `phone` and `email`. Two dropdown changes at `/build/types/organization/edit` make it declared instead of lucky. The case that silently *doesn't* work is a contact field named something less obvious.
> - **Owed: Brandon's ack.** It extends `schema.md`'s property-kind list, so it's core; it's additive-only and his instance behaves identically until he declares a field with one of the new kinds. Raised in `COLLAB.md`.
> - **Three of the sites the backlog note named needed no change, worth recording so nobody hunts for them:** `canvas/FieldStrip.tsx` and `lib/canvas-fields.ts` handle the built-in `items.url` **column** (a link item's address), not the property kind; `lib/item-input.ts` likewise — and properties have **no per-kind write coercion at all**, which is exactly the "store verbatim" behavior the note asked for; `lib/item-tokens.ts` stringifies props kind-agnostically, and a phone number in a `{{item.props.*}}` token should be text, not a link. The real blast radius was the builder pair, `CustomProperties`, `view-where`, and the `ViewRenderer` + four resolution sites.
>
> **Landed:** the `main` build-breaker (an extra named export in `app/page.tsx` — every Vercel deploy from main was failing); the sticky formatting bar (react-grid-layout's `cssTransforms` re-based the sticky containing block; notes were the only type on the grid path); `/items/[id]/markdown` + copy, on every type's ⋯ menu; `settings.itemOpenMode` (auto/left/right/center); tag chips on task rows + group-a-view-by-tag with fan-out; `#` = tags and `+` = project.
>
> **Two resolved by Tyler, not by code:** tags on notes were invisible because the note type's saved `canvas_layout` had `"rel:tags": {hidden: true}` (⋯ → Customize layout → hidden-cards tray restores it); tags on songs and a categories type are user-configurable through the existing relation-field mechanism.
>
> **✅ DONE (2026-08-12, ADR-185) — the `@` create-on-miss picker.** A bare `@Jake Tourtillott` with no match now offers **one typed create row per type it could be** — person, project, then "Unsorted" as the escape hatch — each wearing that type's glyph and naming the type on its right edge. A scoped `@/person Jane` still yields exactly one row (unchanged). Done as **one shared module** (`src/lib/mention-create.ts`) across **five** surfaces, not the three the note named: the editor picker, the capture title, the task card, **plus** the generic "+ Relate" box (`AddRelation.tsx`) and a null-`targetType` relation field (`RelationField.tsx`), which had the same bug in their untyped path. It also settled a pre-existing split the consolidation exposed — the three `@` pickers sent `inbox: true` even for a scoped `@/person Jane` while `RelationField` sent `inbox: !targetType`; now only the catch-all needs triage, in one flippable expression (`needsTriage`). `unmarked` is **not** retired: still the escape hatch, still where quick-capture files. 31 checks in `verify-mention-create.mts` (including a structural guard that a sixth site can't drift back to hardcoding `"unmarked"`), plus end-to-end against the live dev DB. **Not clicked through in a real browser** — no browser automation in that session. The stray "Jake Tourtillott" `unmarked` item can still be retyped from the Inbox.
>
> **✅ DONE (2026-08-12, ADR-186) — the popover collision.** Both of the note's guesses were right and they were **separate** bugs. (1) A **z-index tie across stacking contexts**: the kebab is `absolute z-50` in the page flow, the editor's mention popup was `z-index: 50` on `<body>` — same number, different contexts, so the winner was DOM-order accident, which is what the bleed-through was. Fixed by naming the five tiers the app already used as tokens in `globals.css` (`--z-sticky/chrome/modal/float/top`) and moving both mention popups to the float tier where every other body-portaled typeahead lives. **The rule: a panel portaled to `<body>` belongs at `--z-float` or above, never `--z-modal`.** (2) **Nothing coordinated the two panels** — and the reason is worth keeping: each has its own outside-click listener, but **an outside-click listener never fires for a panel that opens from a keystroke**, which is how `@` opens. `src/lib/floating.ts` is a window-event broadcast (no dependency); all seven top-level floating surfaces announce, the kebab and `Popover` also listen. Sub-popovers inside a menu deliberately don't announce, so "Save as template" still opens inside the kebab. **Found in passing:** `.ledgr-mention-popup` was `position: absolute` but placed from `getBoundingClientRect()` — viewport coords read against the document, so on a scrolled page it landed off by the scroll offset. Now `fixed`. 31 checks in `verify-layering.mts`; the tokens and the rule confirmed in the **served** CSS.
>
> **✅ DONE (2026-08-12, ADR-187) — the two "slight pause" items.** The note was right that both paths were already optimistic, and that's exactly what hid the bug: **the optimistic state stopped half way.** On add, the provisional row kept announcing itself unfinished (`opacity-70`, "Adding…") for the whole ~900ms refresh window when the task had existed since the POST resolved at ~150ms — so `persist()` now returns the real id and the row settles the moment it lands, dropping the muted look and picking up a **real** check circle bound to the real id. On complete, the circle filled instantly but the title's strikethrough read the **server** `statusCategory`, so the completion looked half-applied until the refetch — `TaskCheckCircle` now mirrors its optimistic state outward (`onOptimisticChange`, fired on click *and* on the failure revert) and `TasksWidget` applies it over the server prop. **The 500ms debounce was deliberately NOT shortened** — it exists so a triage burst costs one refetch, and a mid-burst refresh can reorder rows under the pointer; the problem was never that the refetch was late, it was that the UI kept looking pending while it waited. **Deliberately not done:** optimistically *removing* a completed row. `TasksWidget` is the only list rendering this circle and it keeps done tasks (styled `line-through`); whether a completed row should leave is a per-list filter question the shared circle can't answer, so it isn't guessed at. 19 checks in `verify-optimistic-tasks.mts`.
>
> **🧹 TOOL READY, NOT RUN — the throwaway verify owners.** Confirmed **13** of them in the dev DB (`verify-project-*` and `verify-mcp-records-*` `@example.invalid`), owning 23 items, 11 relations and 8 activity events — at most 74 rows. `scripts/purge-throwaway-owners.mjs` removes them, **dry-run by default**; it prints the blast radius and changes nothing until you pass `--apply`. **I did not run `--apply`** — it hard-deletes rather than soft-deleting (this is test residue, not owner content, so Trash isn't the right home), which makes it the one place in the app that deletes outright, so it's your call:
> ```
> node --env-file-if-exists=.env.local scripts/purge-throwaway-owners.mjs          # show
> node --env-file-if-exists=.env.local scripts/purge-throwaway-owners.mjs --apply  # delete
> ```
> It discovers owner-scoped tables and item FKs from the catalog rather than hardcoding a list that would rot, and deletes in FK order (nothing here declares `ON DELETE CASCADE`, so a naive `DELETE FROM users` just fails). Safety: `.invalid` is a reserved TLD (RFC 2606) that can never be a real address, and the pattern also requires the `verify-` prefix, so a real owner cannot match. **Root cause not fixed:** the DB-backed verify scripts create a scratch owner per run and never clean up, so this will refill. The better fix is each script deleting its own owner in a `finally` — worth doing when the 79 DB-backed suites get a runner (see the CI item above).

> **🆕 BACKLOG — the peek/full/arrange window round (Tyler, 2026-08-17; deliberately parked, do NOT fix piecemeal).** Tyler is planning a dedicated round on the item windows — the peek panel, the full-screen view, and the arrange (Customize layout) screen — because "there are a lot of changes that need to be made there." First confirmed bug for that round, with Tyler's own repro: **resizing a link type's URL field to about half width in arrange mode made the URL field disappear entirely from BOTH the peek panel and the full-screen view** — it looked like links had no place to enter a URL at all. Going back into arrange and stretching the field to full width brought it back on both surfaces. So a mid-width card is being dropped from render somewhere in the canvas-layout pipeline (`src/lib/canvas-layout.ts` stores `lg`/`md`/`sm` arrangements; `src/components/canvas/ItemRglInner.tsx` renders them — likely the resized `lg` card never propagated to / got clamped out of the `md`/`sm` layouts, but the cause is untraced, this is just where to look). Related standing item: punch-list item 2 below (Customize-layout-in-a-peek discoverability) belongs to the same round.

> **▶️ PUNCH LIST 2026-08-13/14 (Tyler) — 5 items, causes traced, 4 of 5 SPECIFIED by Tyler. NONE BUILT YET. This is the next build queue.** Each item below carries the cause, the file, and Tyler's decision where he's given one. Two findings changed what the work is, so read 2 and 5 before touching them.
>
> 1. **The project completion bar disappears in a PEEK.** ✅ Specified: Tyler clarified it's the **item peek panel** (`itemOpenMode`), not the nav rail — *"when I open a peek window, the progress bar isn't showing up."* Cause: the track is a hardcoded `bg-neutral-800` at `src/components/projects/ProjectCardGrid.tsx:55` with only the `bg-[var(--accent)]` fill drawn over it, so on a peek surface in the same neutral family the unfilled part vanishes and a 0% project looks bar-less. Fix per ADR-141: a semantic token (`bg-surface-2`/`bg-surface-3`) picked to hold contrast against the peek surface, not a raw gray. Small.
> 2. **"Customize layout" in a peek — 🔎 IT ALREADY DOES WHAT TYLER ASKED FOR; THIS IS A DISCOVERABILITY BUG, NOT A BUILD.** Tyler's ask was *"make two versions, one for the peek and one for the full page… if we use the peek more than the full page we'd never be able to use the customizable layout."* **Those two versions already exist.** `src/lib/canvas-layout.ts:44-58` stores **three** arrangements — `lg`/`md`/`sm` — and the comment maps them explicitly: full-page expand → `lg` (Desktop), **the modal/peek panel (~768px) → `md` (Tablet)**, a phone → `sm`. And `src/components/canvas/ItemRglInner.tsx:519-521` already renders a **Desktop / Tablet / Phone** switcher inside arrange mode, where choosing Tablet clamps the canvas to `maxWidth: 700` — *"≈ the modal's content width, so Tablet is WYSIWYG"* (line 589). So the peek's layout is editable today: Customize layout → **Tablet** → drag. What's actually broken: the menu item is a plain `<a href="/items/<id>?arrange=1">` (`ItemActionsMenu.tsx:227`, hard nav *"so ?arrange=1 escapes the intercept modal"*) that silently teleports you to the full page at Desktop width with no explanation, so it reads as "doesn't work here." **Recommended (needs Tyler's ok): (a) VERIFY in the app that a Tablet-mode edit actually round-trips to the peek — if it doesn't, that's the real bug and fix that instead; (b) make the peek's menu item deep-link to arrange pre-set to Tablet (the width is local state defaulting to `lg`, so this means a URL param); (c) label it so it says it opens full page.** No schema change, so **not core** — Brandon not needed. This is the ADR-189 pattern again: a feature that exists but is unreachable.
> 3. **Task tags go AFTER the title.** ✅ Decided by Tyler: *"tags should always be to the right of the task itself"* — note **always**, so every surface, not just `/tasks`. The previous session predicted this exact reversal and left the hatch: the comment at `src/app/tasks/page.tsx:74-78` says *"moving this one line below the Link is the whole change."* Do that, make the same move in `ViewRenderer`'s row layout so the two agree, and **delete the now-obsolete comment** rather than leaving it contradicting the code. One-liner.
> 4. **Clickable, changeable dates on task rows in the tab views.** ✅ Specified: Tyler wants the date clickable *"in the tabs, like say the 'Today' tab on tasks and such… click the date and change it quickly in context."* The write path already exists (`RowMenu` offers **Schedule** on any `canComplete` row), so this is affordance work, not plumbing. **The one thing still needing an explicit call: ADR-142 says row actions live in `RowMenu` and warns off always-visible per-row controls.** Tyler has effectively asked for the exception; record it as a deliberate carve-out (the date is already drawn, so this makes existing text interactive rather than adding a control) rather than letting it erode the rule silently. **Build it as ONE shared component** (`TaskDateButton`) used by `src/app/tasks/page.tsx`, both `ViewRenderer` layouts (row + table), and the planner rail — five bespoke copies is how this rots. The real feature of the five.
> 5. **Clicking off a task peek opens whatever was underneath** (*"I click to open a task, then click away, and the 'Along the Way' project opens up afterward"*). **Cause fully traced — deliberate behavior producing a surprising result, NOT an event leak.** The peek is non-modal by design (no backdrop, so the list stays live underneath), so `src/components/canvas/Modal.tsx:337-355` listens on the document and decides for itself what counts as "dismiss me." It **refuses to close** when the click lands on `a, button, input, textarea, select, label, summary, [role=button], [role=menuitem], [data-peek-row]` — intent: *"a row still re-navigates the peek to that item."* **The specific trap: `src/components/projects/ProjectCardGrid.tsx:34-75` wraps the ENTIRE project card in one `<Link>`** — padding, progress bar, whitespace, all of it is an anchor. So a click on what reads as background hits an `<a>`, the handler declines to dismiss *and* declines to swallow, and the link navigates. "Along the Way" was just the card under the cursor. Note project cards aren't even `[data-peek-row]` (only `/notes`, `/links`, `/list/[type]` carry that), so the intentional row-hopping feature isn't what caught him. **⏭️ AWAITING TYLER'S PICK — he asked to discuss and hasn't chosen:** **(A)** any outside click dismisses and swallows, `[data-peek-row]` the lone exception so triage row-hopping survives — costs two clicks to act on anything underneath, nav included; **(B)** = A but clicks inside the nav still pass through in one click (**recommended**); **(C)** just narrow the bail-out to `[data-peek-row]` so buttons/fields underneath still work — smallest, but leaves the "large link reads as background" class of surprise partly alive. Adding a backdrop would fix it trivially and was **rejected**: it would kill the live-list-underneath design intent.
>

> **✅ DONE (2026-08-14, Tyler) — 56 new nav icons (62 → 118) and the task-canvas subtask/rail cleanup. NOT CLICKED THROUGH IN A BROWSER.** Two unrelated asks in one session, both merged as one change. `tsc`, `eslint`, and the grouping invariant are clean; nothing here is core, so no ADR and no `COLLAB.md` entry.
>
> **The icons (`src/lib/nav-icons.ts`, 118 keys, 4 new picker groups).** The trigger was a new `Organization` type with no glyph, and the ask was explicitly *"so we don't have to make the icons one by one."* Added in two passes: everyday subjects (organization, church, place, briefcase, money, receipt, chart, cart, package, email, phone, chat, megaphone, quote, video, camera, mic, laptop, code, database, car, plane, gift, utensils, dumbbell, leaf, paw, cross, check, checklist, key, lock, shield, lightbulb, pin, paperclip, alert, info, sparkle), then the gaps that left (contract, invoice, store, door, floorplan, gear, repeat, sun, cloud, plant, pill, headphones, broadcast, playlist, family, open-book, scripture). New groups: **Places & Business, Communication, Media & Tech, Life**; `key`/`lock`/`shield`/`lightbulb`/`pin`/`paperclip`/`gear`/`repeat` went into Tools, the status marks into Misc.
> - **The verification method is the reusable part, worth stealing next time.** Hand-rolled SVG path strings are unreviewable as text, so each pass was rendered to a PNG grid and actually looked at, using the **Playwright chromium already cached at `~/Library/Caches/ms-playwright/`** driven as `chrome-headless-shell --headless --screenshot`. No new dependency (Principle 5), and it caught four glyphs that were fine as code and wrong as pictures: `car` read as a bus, `pin` as a blob, `quote` as the letters "GG", `utensils` as a tuning fork. All four were redrawn and re-rendered.
> - **`handshake` was attempted and DROPPED, deliberately.** Four versions, none legible as two clasped hands at 24px in a 1.8 stroke (they read as a squiggle or a mushroom). `contract` and `store` cover the vendor/partner sense. **The reason is recorded in a comment in the file** so nobody re-attempts it blind; if someone wants it, it probably needs a filled glyph, not a stroke one.
> - **The grouping invariant is checked, not assumed:** every key in `NAV_ICONS` appears in exactly one `NAV_ICON_GROUPS` entry (118 grouped, 0 ungrouped, 0 duplicated). **There is no test enforcing this**, only the comment above the constant, so a future addition can silently fall out of the picker. A tiny pure verify script would close it.
>
> **The task canvas (`TaskCanvas.tsx` + 6 files), against a Todoist reference Tyler supplied.** Five fixes, every one scoped behind a `bare` prop rather than changed globally, because all of these components are shared with the stacked default canvas where the current treatment is right:
> - **Rail 340px → 280px.** It carries short rows; the extra 60px only stole reading width from the title/body/subtask pane.
> - **Rail property labels now stack ABOVE their values** (`CustomProperties`, `RelationProperties`, `PeopleRow`, which needed a new `bare` prop). They held a fixed `w-32` (128px) label column, which on a 280px rail squeezed every value into a truncated sliver. **The wide canvas keeps the side-by-side column.**
> - **"Add subtask" brightened** to `text-ink` (was `text-neutral-600`, nearly invisible). "Add existing" went to `text-ink-muted` so the pair reads primary/secondary instead of one bright and one lost.
> - **The "SUBTASKS" header and the over-indentation were the same bug.** Both came from the `CanvasSection` wrapper, which also applies `px-2 sm:px-8 md:px-12` reading-column padding, which is what pushed subtasks far right inside an already-narrowed pane. A `bare` prop on `Subtasks` drops the whole frame; nested subtasks keep a gentler step (`ml-2 pl-2.5`, was `ml-4 pl-3`).
> - **The "two thin white lines" were two different borders**, which is why one fix wouldn't have done it: the section's `border-top` (gone with the header) and the **editor wrapper's `border-b`** at `MarkdownEditor.tsx`, now gated on the existing `compact` flag.
> - **⚠️ THAT LAST ONE HAS A WIDER BLAST RADIUS THAN THE TASK CANVAS.** `compact` is also set by `WidgetCanvas.tsx` and `dashboards/EmbedWidget.tsx`, so both lose their bottom rule too. Judged an improvement (all three are inline editors sitting above other content, not full-height documents), but it was **not** what Tyler asked for and he has not seen it. Revert to a dedicated prop threaded `ItemEditor → BodyEditor → MarkdownEditor` if either surface looks wrong.
> - **The "n of m done" rollup moved,** it lived on the header that got removed. Rather than lose it, it now rides the right edge of the add row. Drop it if that reads as clutter.
>
> **⏭️ FOLLOW-UPS, none started:** (1) **the event canvas shares `CanvasTwoPane`** and still has the wide rail and the `w-32` label column, so it now looks different from tasks for no reason a user could explain, the same class of inconsistency ADR-141 exists to prevent; (2) a verify script for the icon-grouping invariant; (3) `handshake` as a filled glyph if it's ever actually wanted. **And the honest caveat: the canvas work is code-verified only.** `tsc` and `eslint` pass and the reasoning traces to specific lines, but no browser touched it, and layout is exactly the category where that isn't proof. Sixty seconds on a real task with subtasks and a couple of relation fields would settle it.

> **✅ DONE (2026-08-14, ADR-194) — instances can update THEMSELVES: Build → Updates.** Built for Michelle (GitHub `michellecollins1212-rosanne`, account created 2026-08-14) but it answers Tyler's question too, and the two are **not the same question**. New page `/build/updates` reports two independent axes: **code** (are you running the latest Ledgr) and **schema** (has your database caught up with the code you're running). A satellite instance — one deploying from a **fork** — gets a list of what changed and an **Update now** button that calls GitHub's `merge-upstream` on its own fork, then polls until the rebuild takes over. A **source** instance (Tyler's, Brandon's) is told plainly it already receives every push, so no dead button. Satellite-ness is derived from Vercel's own git env vars, so **a satellite needs no configuration to be identified**.
>
> - **The schema axis is the one that matters for Tyler.** His Vercel deploys from `strategicli/ledgr` (confirmed: `prod-tyler` does **not** exist on origin, so main is his production branch), so his code is never behind. His **database** silently can be. Proved live mid-session: `origin/main` moved **4 commits** ahead (PRs #256 offline page, #257 export fix) while this was being built. Those carried **no migrations**, so nothing was owed, but that is luck, not a guarantee. The journal is a **static import**, so it ships in the bundle and the check needs no network.
> - **`LEDGR_SELF_UPDATE` gates the button and fails closed:** `off` (default) status-only, `safe` allows schema-free updates, `on` allows any — and **`on` is only correct with Build Command `npm run build:satellite`** (migrate, then build), where a bad migration fails the build and Vercel keeps the last working deploy live. The verify script **caught this gate failing open** before it shipped.
> - **Two ops scripts for what the button can't cover:** `npm run instance:new -- <name>` collapses the four provisioning steps (migrate → seed → owner row → verify) whose omission produces the ADR-184 "Signed in, but not recognized" screen; `npm run instances:sync` migrates each DB **first** then syncs each fork, refusing to sync one whose migration failed. Roster in gitignored `instances.local.json`.
> - **Verified:** 36 pure checks (`verify-updates.mts`, now in CI — 56 pure suites), live schema check green against dev (50/50), `verify:ci` all green, tsc/eslint/build clean. **NOT verified end to end: no fork exists yet**, so `merge-upstream`, the deploy poll, and the whole satellite code path have never run for real. No browser touched the page. Runbook §1k has the setup order.
> - **⏭️ NEXT, in order:** (1) Michelle forks `strategicli/ledgr` from her own account (a fork lands in whoever clicks it — Tyler **cannot** do it for her, and cannot invite her to the shared repo either: he is an org `member` with `push`, not `admin`); (2) her Vercel connects to **her fork**; (3) `npm run instance:new -- michelle`; (4) set her env vars, **her own Clerk app**; (5) she signs in; (6) then set `LEDGR_SELF_UPDATE` + `build:satellite` and prove the button on a real update. Also still open from 2026-08-13: **delete the pointless `prod-miles` branch** (it still exists on origin).

>
> - **🆕 2026-08-17 (Tyler): Miles gets the READ-ONLY update posture.** Same install as Michelle's except the self-update write path is left off: `LEDGR_SELF_UPDATE` stays **unset** (Updates page is status-only, no button), and his `GITHUB_TOKEN`, if set at all, is Contents: **read-only** (Changelog + update status still work; only the Update button needs write). Updates reach him via `npm run instances:sync -- --only miles` (Miles adds Tyler as a collaborator on HIS fork) or GitHub's own "Sync fork" button, which `build:satellite` makes safe. The full step-by-step is `~/Downloads/ledgr-setup-for-miles.md`. As of this date his fork still does not exist (verified via the API); his GitHub account does.
